\texttt{dynamic\_sampling}      &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 1 \\
\texttt{kl\_coef}               &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 2 (ref/KL) \\
\texttt{kl\_estimator}          &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 2 \\
\texttt{ratio\_level}           &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 1 \\
\texttt{ref\_snapshot}          &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 2 \\
\texttt{token\_mask}            &    0.0 & 0.0000 & Item 1 \\
\midrule
\texttt{decoding\_parameters}   &  100.0 & 0.0000 & Item 3 (sampler) \\
\texttt{precision}              &  100.0 & 0.0000 & Item 3 \\
\texttt{sampler\_backend}       &  100.0 & 0.0000 & Item 3 \\
\texttt{sampling\_engine}       &  100.0 & 0.0000 & Item 3 \\
\midrule
\texttt{decontam\_check}        &  100.0 & 0.9313 & Item 7 \\
\texttt{parser\_probe}          &  100.0 & 0.9313 & Item 7 \\
\texttt{disjointness}           &  100.0 & 1.5546 & Item 6 (held-out) \\
\texttt{split\_name}            &  100.0 & 1.5546 & Item 6 \\
\texttt{split\_size}            &  100.0 & 1.5546 & Item 6 \\

The sub-field split is structurally clean. The 9 Item-1 (loss form)
sub-fields and Item-2 (reference policy \& KL) sub-fields score
$0/98$---the manifest reports only \texttt{"n/a-sampling"} and
\texttt{"n/a"} for these items, indistinguishable from emission
failures. The 4 Item-3 (sampler/backend/precision) sub-fields score
$100/98$ but with $H{=}0$: every cell reports \texttt{"tinker-closed"}.
The remaining 9 sub-fields (Items 4--7) carry $H {>} 0$ and
informationally distinguish cells. Across the 22 sub-fields,
\textbf{$13/22$ ($59\%$) carry no information} and only $9/22$
($41\%$) are information-bearing.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{../experiments/results/p5p8/figures/p5_minreport_subfield_audit.png}
\caption{Per-sub-field coverage (left) and minimum-viable-extension lift
(right) on the $n{=}98$ cell mega corpus. Left: 9 sub-fields have
$0\%$ coverage (grey), 4 are vacuous at $100\%$ coverage with $H{=}0$
(dark green), 9 carry information (light green). Right: adding a
single continuous-telemetry field to MIN-REPORT alone lifts distinct
profiles from $15/98$ to $98/98$ (the
\texttt{mean\_completion\_len} bar); \texttt{task\_slice}, $G$,
\texttt{n\_groups}, and \texttt{sample\_errors} contribute zero
incremental lift because they are already captured in Items 5 and 6.}
\label{fig:p5-subfield-audit}
\end{figure}

The second part of this exhibit measures the gap between the current
seven-field run manifest and the information it actually carries at the
\emph{cell} (joined) level. Two baselines are computed:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Raw}---joined string including \texttt{per\_step\_zvf\_path}.
\item \textbf{Content}---joined string \emph{excluding}
  \texttt{per\_step\_zvf\_path}, which carries one unique path per cell
  and would otherwise dominate any distinctness statistic.
\end{itemize}
On the raw baseline the seven manifest fields yield $98/98$ distinct profiles
($H{=}6.6147$ bits, i.e., the maximum $\log_2 98$); on the content
baseline they yield only \textbf{$15/98$ distinct profiles}
($H{=}3.7780$ bits). The 5.6-fold ratio between the two baselines is
the quantitative counterpart to Exhibit~17's observation that
\texttt{per\_step\_zvf\_path} is an opaque file-pointer carrying the
cell identity, not a stack-property.

The minimum-viable-extension (MVE) analysis ranks each candidate field
in \texttt{cells.tsv} by the number of additional distinct profiles it
adds to the content baseline. The result is a striking
\emph{single-field} MVE: adding \texttt{mean\_completion\_len} (or
\texttt{std\_completion\_len}) lifts the distinct profile count from
$15/98$ to $98/98$ (a 6.5x lift, $\Delta H = +2.84$ bits). Among the
two top continuous-telemetry fields, $98/98$ cells become uniquely
identifiable. Ranked lower:
$\texttt{mean\_reward}$ (+52 profiles), \texttt{pcd} (+51),
\texttt{zvf} (+40), \texttt{seed} (+15), \texttt{model\_family}
(+13), \texttt{temperature} (+12). The fields already implicit in the
seven manifest fields (\texttt{task\_slice}, $G$, \texttt{n\_groups},
\texttt{sample\_errors}) contribute \textbf{0 incremental profiles}
beyond the content baseline, confirming that the seven run-manifest fields
exhaust the categorical stack information but \emph{miss the
continuous-telemetry layer} that dominates run-to-run variance.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\small
\caption{Minimum-viable-extension (MVE) lift on the $n{=}98$ content
baseline of $15/98$ distinct MIN-REPORT profiles. Rank 1--12 by
$\Delta$ distinct. Adding a single continuous-telemetry field (rank 1)
lifts the distinct profile count to $98/98$. Repeated noise from the
\texttt{cells.tsv} categorical columns (ranks 9--12) is already
captured by the existing seven manifest fields.}
\label{tab:p5-mve}
\begin{tabular}{@{}clrrr@{}}
\toprule
Rank & Extension field & $\Delta$ distinct & Cumul.\ distinct & Cumul.\ lift \% \\
\midrule
1 & \texttt{mean\_completion\_len} & 83 & 98/98 & 84.7 \\
2 & \texttt{std\_completion\_len}  & 83 & 98/98 & 84.7 \\
3 & \texttt{mean\_reward}         & 52 & 67/98 & 53.1 \\
4 & \texttt{pcd}                  & 51 & 66/98 & 52.0 \\
5 & \texttt{zvf}                  & 40 & 55/98 & 40.8 \\
6 & \texttt{seed}                 & 15 & 30/98 & 15.3 \\
7 & \texttt{model\_family}        & 13 & 28/98 & 13.3 \\
8 & \texttt{temperature}          & 12 & 27/98 & 12.2 \\
9 & \texttt{task\_slice}           &  0 & 15/98 &  0.0 \\
10& $G$                           &  0 & 15/98 &  0.0 \\
11& \texttt{n\_groups}            &  0 & 15/98 &  0.0 \\
12& \texttt{sample\_errors}       &  0 & 15/98 &  0.0 \\
\bottomrule

\end{table}

\paragraph{Actionable recommendation.}
The seven MIN-REPORT run-manifest fields are sufficient for the categorical
stack axes (\textit{what} model, \textit{what} task, \textit{what}
group-size schedule, \textit{what} held-out split). They are
\emph{incomplete} for the continuous-telemetry layer (\textit{how
much} mean / std reward, ZVF, PCD, completion length). Adding a
single optional key \texttt{mean\_completion\_len} (or any one
telemetry-channel mean) to the manifest format lifts the per-cell
distinct profile count from $15\%$ to $100\%$ on this corpus. We
recommend the maintainers of the MIN-REPORT-RL standard accept the
\textbf{continuous-telemetry layer} (\texttt{mean\_reward},
\texttt{zvf}, \texttt{pcd}, \texttt{mean\_completion\_len},
\texttt{std\_completion\_len}) as a \emph{recommended but not yet
required} telemetry extension. This recommendation is a sharp quantification
of the legacy iter-32 expansion-to-18-fields suggestion, restricted to the
five sub-fields that measurably break the iter-52 per-cell vacuum. It is
orthogonal to the current standard's Item~8, which reports pass@$k$ curves.

\paragraph{Reproduction.}
\texttt{python3 scripts/p5p8/p5\_minreport\_subfield\_audit.py}
($\sim$2\,s, stdlib only). Outputs:
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_minreport\_subfield\_audit.tsv}
(22 rows), \texttt{p5\_minreport\_subfield\_audit\_per\_item.tsv}
(7 rows),
\texttt{p5\_minreport\_subfield\_audit\_summary.json},
\texttt{p5\_minreport\_mve.tsv} (12 rows),
\texttt{p5\_minreport\_mve\_summary.json};
figure script
\texttt{scripts/p5p8/fig\_p5\_minreport\_subfield.py} produces
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/figures/p5\_minreport\_subfield\_audit.\{png,pdf\}}.

\subsection{Exhibit 19: Empirical validation of the iter-53 MVE recommendation}
\label{sec:p5-mve-empirical}

Exhibit 18 (\secref{sec:p5-subfield-audit}) showed that the
principled-minimum 5-field extension
($\{$mean\_reward, zvf, pcd, mean\_completion\_len,
std\_completion\_len$\}$) lifts distinct manifest profiles
$15 \rightarrow 98$ on the 98-cell mega corpus. That analysis was
\emph{theoretical}---it operated on the manifest's declared field
values. This exhibit validates the recommendation \emph{empirically}
by reading the actual measured values from
\texttt{mega\_20260704/cells.tsv} and computing the badge-mean uplift
on the canonical auditor formula.

\textbf{Method.} For each of the 98 mega manifests we (i) score the
seven-field MIN-REPORT run manifest using the iter-13 auditor formula
($\sum_{i=1}^{7} w_i \cdot \mathrm{base}_i \cdot \mathrm{sub\_frac}_i$,
weights summing to $100$), and (ii) score a historical candidate
telemetry extension (weight $w_T$), whose
sub-field coverage is the number of populated numeric values among
the 5 MVE fields. We compute per-cell $\Delta\mathrm{badge} =
\mathrm{augmented} - \mathrm{baseline}$ and report the mean $\Delta$
with paired bootstrap $B{=}2{,}000$, seed $20260704$.

\textbf{Headline finding (deterministic).} All $98/98$ cells in the
canonical mega corpus have \emph{all five} MVE fields populated in
\texttt{cells.tsv} (verified by direct query: $98$ rows, every cell
has a non-empty numeric value for each of mean\_reward, zvf, pcd,
mean\_completion\_len, std\_completion\_len). The MVE extension
therefore delivers a \emph{constant} badge uplift on every cell:
$\Delta = +20.00$ at full weight ($w_T = 20$) and
$\Delta = +10.00$ at half weight ($w_T = 10$). The $95\%$ bootstrap CI
collapses to $[+20.00, +20.00]$ (and $[+10.00, +10.00]$ at half
weight)---degenerate because there is no variance.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Empirical badge uplift from adding the iter-53 candidate
continuous-telemetry extension to the seven-field MIN-REPORT run manifest.
Every cell in
the 98-cell corpus already populates all five MVE fields in
\texttt{cells.tsv}, so the uplift is deterministic (CI collapses).}
\label{tab:p5-mve-empirical}
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
\toprule
Scenario & $w_T$ & Baseline & Augmented & $\Delta$ & 95\% CI & Excl.\ 0 \\
\midrule
Full weight  & $20$ & $90.0$ & $110.0$ & $+20.0$ & $[+20.0, +20.0]$ & \textbf{YES} \\
Half weight  & $10$ & $90.0$ & $100.0$ & $+10.0$ & $[+10.0, +10.0]$ & \textbf{YES} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Sharpest reviewer-facing falsifiable claim.}
The iter-53 MVE recommendation is not theoretical---it is a single
schema bump that delivers a deterministic $+20$ badge uplift on every
one of the 98 mega cells, because every cell already populates all
five MVE fields in the canonical telemetry table. The
iter-52 honest-but-vacuous diagnosis was a structural artifact of
the seven-field MIN-REPORT run manifest's dependence on structured-string fields that
collapse to a single value; the fix is the one-item continuous-telemetry
extension.

\paragraph{Cross-coupling.} This exhibit closes the iter-53 \#64
``next-step recommendation'' and validates iter-32's load-bearing
finding (the load-bearing fields are the continuous-telemetry set,
not the structured-string set) end-to-end on the corpus. It also
sharpens iter-52 \#63's per-cell 3-axis triangulation: the
honest-but-vacuous classification is solvable in one schema bump, not
a corpus-wide fix.

\paragraph{Reproduction.}
\texttt{python3 scripts/p5p8/p5\_mve\_empirical.py} ($\sim$10s on 4
cores). Outputs:
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_mve\_empirical.tsv} (98 rows per-cell
baseline + augmented + low-weight),
\texttt{p5\_mve\_empirical\_summary.json}.

\subsection{Exhibit 20: Candidate telemetry-extension field-level distributional sanity audit}
\label{sec:p5-mve-field-audit}

Exhibit 19 (\secref{sec:p5-mve-empirical}) validated the iter-53
sub-field MVE recommendation end-to-end on the empirical \texttt{cells.tsv}:
all 5 MVE fields are populated on every cell, lifting distinct profiles
$15 \rightarrow 98$ and delivering a deterministic $+20$ badge
uplift. This exhibit moves \emph{one level deeper}, from the aggregate
5-tuple to per-field distributional sanity: does each of the 5 MVE
fields carry independent information, and is the per-field
stack-conditioning strong enough to license the candidate telemetry
extension at field-level granularity?

\textbf{Method.} On the 98-cell mega corpus, for each of the 5 MVE
fields
($\{\mathrm{mean\_reward}, \mathrm{zvf}, \mathrm{pcd},
\mathrm{mean\_completion\_len}, \mathrm{std\_completion\_len}\}$),
we compute (i) the empirical distribution
($\min / \max / \mathrm{median} / \mathrm{IQR} / \sigma / \%$-NaN /
\%-saturated), (ii) one-way $\eta^2$ on each of the 5 stack axes
($\mathrm{model\_family}, \mathrm{task\_slice}, G,
\mathrm{temperature}, \mathrm{seed}$) with block-bootstrap 95\% CI
($B{=}2{,}000$, seed $20260704$, cell-level resample with
replacement), and (iii) the cross-axis sum $\eta^2$ (sum of the 5
axis-level $\eta^2$ values per field). Per-pair Pearson $r$ on the 5
fields is computed on aligned cells ($n{=}98$). Per-cell 5-tuple
fingerprint uniqueness and outlier flags
($\mathrm{zvf} \in \{0,1\}, \mathrm{reward} \in \{0,1\}, \sigma = 0$)
are reported jointly.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\small
\caption{Per-MVE-field cross-axis $\eta^2$ decomposition (5 fields
$\times$ 5 stack axes, $n{=}98$ cells, block-bootstrap 95\% CI
$B{=}2{,}000$ seed $20260704$). All five fields PASS the
$0.50$ stack-dominance threshold (cross-axis sum
$\geq 0.7310$), and each field's dominant axis is DIFFERENT —
the 5-tuple is not a redundant re-encoding. $\mathrm{seed}$ is
near-zero on every field (upper CI $\leq 0.061$), confirming the
iter-32 noise-axis finding at per-MVE-field granularity.}
\label{tab:p5-mve-field}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
Field & $\eta^2_{\mathrm{model}}$ & $\eta^2_{\mathrm{task}}$ &
$\eta^2_G$ & $\eta^2_{\mathrm{temp}}$ & $\eta^2_{\mathrm{seed}}$ \\
\midrule
$\mathrm{mean\_reward}$ &
$0.453\,[0.304,0.628]$ & $0.273\,[0.190,0.414]$ &
$0.030\,[0.011,0.174]$ & $0.000\,[0.000,0.055]$ &
$0.000\,[0.000,0.057]$ \\
$\mathrm{zvf}$ &
$0.005\,[0.000,0.070]$ & $0.469\,[0.373,0.598]$ &
$0.444\,[0.277,0.636]$ & $0.009\,[0.000,0.081]$ &
$0.000\,[0.000,0.051]$ \\
$\mathrm{pcd}$ &
$0.093\,[0.015,0.215]$ & $0.451\,[0.362,0.579]$ &
$0.230\,[0.125,0.426]$ & $0.009\,[0.000,0.090]$ &
$0.001\,[0.000,0.057]$ \\
$\mathrm{mean\_comp\_len}$ &
$0.301\,[0.176,0.439]$ & $0.210\,[0.081,0.406]$ &
$0.017\,[0.008,0.145]$ & $0.207\,[0.084,0.386]$ &
$0.000\,[0.000,0.056]$ \\
$\mathrm{std\_comp\_len}$ &
$0.159\,[0.052,0.319]$ & $0.413\,[0.291,0.569]$ &
$0.034\,[0.011,0.176]$ & $0.123\,[0.026,0.294]$ &
$0.002\,[0.000,0.061]$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{Headline finding 1 — per-field cross-axis $\eta^2$ all exceed
$0.50$.} $\mathrm{zvf}$ leads with $0.927$ (task + G dominate), then
$\mathrm{pcd}$ $0.784$, $\mathrm{mean\_reward}$ $0.756$,
$\mathrm{mean\_comp\_len}$ $0.734$, $\mathrm{std\_comp\_len}$
$0.731$. The candidate telemetry extension's stack conditioning is preserved at
per-field granularity: each field is at least $73\%$ stack-explained,
and every field's upper CI bound on the dominant axis is
$>\!0.50$.

\textbf{Headline finding 2 — the 5-tuple is not redundant.} Each
field has a \emph{different} dominant axis:
$\mathrm{mean\_reward} \leftarrow \mathrm{model}$;
$\mathrm{zvf} \leftarrow \mathrm{task} + G$;
$\mathrm{pcd} \leftarrow \mathrm{task}$;
$\mathrm{mean\_comp\_len} \leftarrow \mathrm{model} + \mathrm{temp}$;
$\mathrm{std\_comp\_len} \leftarrow \mathrm{task} + \mathrm{model}$.
The pairwise Pearson $r$ matrix confirms the non-redundancy: the
strongest pair is $\mathrm{zvf} \leftrightarrow \mathrm{pcd}$
at $r = -0.888$ (anti-correlated, both bounded $[0,1]$); every other
pair has $|r| < 0.83$, and 8/10 pairs have $|r| < 0.67$. The
5-tuple measures \emph{different} signals, not the same signal five
ways.

\textbf{Headline finding 3 — $\mathrm{seed}$ is the noise axis on
every field.} Per-field $\eta^2_{\mathrm{seed}}$ upper CI
$\leq 0.061$ on all 5 fields (max $0.061$ on
$\mathrm{std\_comp\_len}$, min $0.051$ on $\mathrm{zvf}$). Once
$\mathrm{model} + \mathrm{task} + G + \mathrm{temperature}$ are
fixed, the seed moves every MVE field by $\leq 6\%$ of variance.
This is the per-MVE-field re-statement of iter-32's
load-bearing finding that \texttt{seed} is a nuisance axis on the
disclosed stack.

\textbf{Headline finding 4 — $100\%$ unique 5-tuples.}
Every one of the 98 mega cells carries a distinct 5-tuple
($\mathrm{max\_dup\_count} = 1$). The iter-53
``distinct profiles $15 \rightarrow 98$'' cardinality claim is
recovered at per-cell granularity: on the empirical
\texttt{cells.tsv}, no two cells share the same 5-tuple.

\textbf{Headline finding 5 — $\mathrm{humaneval\_subset}$ is
completely degenerate on 3/5 fields.} On the 34
$\mathrm{humaneval\_subset}$ cells, $\mathrm{mean\_reward} \equiv 0$,
$\mathrm{zvf} \equiv 1$, $\mathrm{pcd} \equiv 0$ (zero variance on
each). The 34 cells still distinguish themselves on
$\mathrm{mean\_comp\_len}$ ($\sigma = 66.6$) and
$\mathrm{std\_comp\_len}$ ($\sigma = 19.1$). The 5-tuple on
$\mathrm{humaneval\_subset}$ reduces to a 2-tuple
($\mathrm{length}, \mathrm{std}$) plus 3 constants -- the iter-53
``distinct profiles'' headroom is dominantly driven by the
humaneval cells splitting on length.

\textbf{Headline finding 6 — outlier density.}
$36/98$ cells ($36.7\%$) have $\mathrm{zvf} \in \{0, 1\}$
(saturated); $35/98$ ($35.7\%$) have
$\mathrm{mean\_reward} \in \{0,1\}$ (all on
$\mathrm{humaneval\_subset}$). $0/98$ cells have zero
$\mathrm{std\_comp\_len}$ -- the standard-deviation field is
universally non-degenerate. The $36/98$ zvf-saturation is the
operational manifestation of iter-49's
``zvf is determined by task + G'' finding: G=2 on
gsm8k\_easy + Llama-3.2-3B at temperature 0.6/1.0 has 32/35 cells
at zvf $\geq 0.95$ (saturated).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\linewidth]{p5_mve_field_dist.png}
\caption{Candidate telemetry-extension MVE field-level audit on the 98 mega cells. Top-left
4 panels: histograms of the 4 bounded MVE fields
($\mathrm{mean\_reward}, \mathrm{zvf}, \mathrm{pcd},
\mathrm{mean\_completion\_len}, \mathrm{std\_completion\_len}$);
bottom-left: cross-axis $\eta^2$ sum per field (red dashed line
$=0.50$ threshold). All 5 fields exceed the threshold; $\mathrm{zvf}$
leads at $0.927$.}
\label{fig:p5-mve-field}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Sharpest reviewer-facing falsifiable claim.} The iter-53 MVE
recommendation is not a single bulk addition: it is a 5-tuple of
\emph{distinct} signal-bearing fields, each driven by a \emph{different}
stack axis, and each with $\geq 73\%$ stack conditioning on the 98-cell
mega corpus. The 5-tuple achieves $100\%$ per-cell uniqueness;
$\mathrm{humaneval\_subset}$ reduces the 5-tuple to a 2-tuple on 34
cells, but the remaining 64 cells preserve the full 5-tuple
information. The candidate telemetry extension is therefore
\emph{field-heterogeneous} in the strongest sense: its five-field block is
load-bearing in this diagnostic, and the load is unevenly distributed across
those fields. This does not evaluate the current standard's pass@$k$ Item~8.

\textbf{Cross-coupling.} This exhibit closes iter-53 \#64
sub-field MVE at the per-FIELD level, sharpens iter-56 \#67
empirical MVE validation by reporting per-field structure (not just
aggregate badge uplift), re-states iter-32 \#32's noise-axis
finding at per-MVE-field granularity (every field's
$\eta^2_{\mathrm{seed}}$ upper CI $\leq 0.061$), and sharpens
iter-49 \#60 stack-conditioning mega (which used only 2 of the 5
MVE outcomes) to all 5 fields.

\textbf{Reproduction.}
\texttt{python3 scripts/p5p8/p5\_mve\_field\_audit.py}
($\sim$3s, stdlib + matplotlib). Outputs:
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_mve\_field\_audit.tsv} (5 rows
per-field distribution),
\texttt{p5\_mve\_field\_eta2.tsv} (5 fields $\times$ 6 axis rows:
5 axes + cross\_sum),
\texttt{p5\_mve\_field\_corr.tsv} (5$\times$5 Pearson matrix),
\texttt{p5\_mve\_field\_summary.json},
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/figures/p5\_mve\_field\_dist.\{png,pdf\}}.

\subsection{Exhibit 21: Algorithm-vs-stack ratio robustness --- the iter-193 headline survives axis-selection but is axis-conditional}
\label{sec:p5-exhibit-ratio-robustness}

Exhibit~9 reported bootstrap CIs on the algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ on each
channel (3 -- 6.3\%); Exhibit~5 reported the algorithm-vs-stack ratio at
point estimate. Neither audit addressed two weaknesses: (i) iter-193's
``top stack axis'' was selected post-hoc from five candidates (a
multiple-comparisons bias that overstates the headline); (ii) iter-193
bootstrapped each axis independently, but never computed a bootstrap CI on
the \emph{ratio} (a distinct statistic). Exhibit~21 closes both gaps with
four paired-bootstrap stress tests ($B{=}2000$, seed 20260706, 95\%
percentile CI).

\paragraph{Falsifiable headline.}
For \textsc{ZVF} and \textsc{reward}, the paired-bootstrap CI on
$\eta^2_\text{stack}/\eta^2_\text{algo}$ excludes $1.0$ on at least one
stack axis \emph{and} the dominant stack axes
(\texttt{model\_family}, \texttt{task\_slice}, $G$) each individually
carry the signal. If the headline collapses when the worst axis is
chosen instead of the best, iter-193 is a single-axis cherry-pick. If it
survives, the headline is robust.

\paragraph{Method.}
For each of $15$ (channel $\times$ stack axis) cells,
we resample WITHIN-LEVEL (stratified) on both the algorithm axis
(N2, $4$ GRPO-family methods, $40$ steps each) and the stack axis
(mega, $98$ cells, $k=2$--$5$ levels per axis). On every resample we
recompute $\eta^2_\text{algo}$ and $\eta^2_\text{stack}$, and track the
distribution of the ratio $r = \eta^2_\text{stack} / \eta^2_\text{algo}$.
The 95\% CI is the percentile interval of the $r$ distribution. We also
run (a) a worst-axis stress (pick the axis with the smallest
$\eta^2$); (b) a one-axis-dropped jackknife (drop one of the five
stack axes, take the worst of the remaining four); (c) a multi-axis
composite ($\eta^2$ on the additive main-effects prediction = mean of
per-axis group-mean predictions; Cohen 1973 / Hays 1973) for both the
full 5-axis and the 3-dominant-axis subsets.

\paragraph{Headline.}
\tableref{tab:p5-iter197-ratio-robustness} summarises the 15 paired-bootstrap
cells.

\textbf{Headline finding 1 --- the headline survives axis-selection for
the dominant axes (H1--H3 PASS).} On \textsc{ZVF} the ratio CI excludes
$1.0$ for \texttt{task\_slice} (CI $[3.33, 52.61]$) and $G$ (CI $[2.91,
43.52]$), and excludes $3.0$ for \texttt{task\_slice} alone. On
\textsc{reward} the CI excludes $1.0$ for \texttt{model\_family} (CI
$[5.62, 247.88]$), \texttt{task\_slice} (CI $[3.52, 184.14]$), and $G$ (CI
$[0.37, 40.95]$). Every dominant axis carries the signal.

\textbf{Headline finding 2 --- the signal propagates, not a single-axis
artifact (H5' PASS).} Drop a dominant axis and another dominant axis
still excludes $1.0$: drop \texttt{task\_slice} $\Rightarrow G$ still
excludes $1$ on \textsc{ZVF} (CI $[2.91, 43.52]$); drop
\texttt{model\_family} $\Rightarrow$ \texttt{task\_slice} still excludes
$1$ on \textsc{reward} (CI $[3.52, 184.14]$); drop $G$ $\Rightarrow$
\texttt{task\_slice} still excludes $1$ on \textsc{ZVF} (CI $[3.33,
52.61]$).

\textbf{Headline finding 3 --- noisy axes are degenerate (H4 PASS,
scope clarification).} The two noisy axes (\texttt{seed},
\texttt{temperature}) have $\eta^2 \approx 0$ on every channel and their
paired-bootstrap CI \emph{includes} $1.0$. The label-vs-stack comparison
is degenerate when the stack axis is not varied in the experiment.

\textbf{Headline finding 4 --- naive averaging dilutes the signal (H6
FAIL, scope clarification).} The naive 5-axis additive composite gives
$\eta^2_\text{composite} = 0.0441$ (\textsc{ZVF}, ratio $=0.97\times$,
CI $[0.32, 5.17]$) and $0.0316$ (\textsc{reward}, ratio $=4.23\times$,
CI $[0.47, 18.69]$); the CI includes $1.0$ on \textsc{ZVF}. The 3-dominant-axis composite gives $\eta^2 = 0.1211$ (\textsc{ZVF}, ratio
$=2.67\times$, CI $[0.84, 14.52]$) and $0.0877$ (\textsc{reward},
ratio $=11.75\times$, CI $[1.20, 52.34]$, excludes $1$). Naively
averaging noisy axes dilutes the signal because they contribute zero
unique variance; averaging correlated dominant axes also dilutes (the
three dominant axes share variance through task-stratification).

\textbf{Sharp reading.} The iter-193 ``stack dominates label''
headline is \emph{not} a single-axis cherry-pick (H5' PASS) but is
\emph{axis-conditional} (H4 PASS, H6 FAIL): the ratio holds for axes that
are actually varied in the experimental design (\texttt{task\_slice},
\texttt{model\_family}, $G$) and degenerates for axes that are not
(\texttt{seed}, \texttt{temperature}). The ratio is a property of the
\emph{experimental design}, not a universal constant of RL-for-LLM
stacks. This sharpens Exhibit~7's stack-axis $\eta^2$ summary: the
variance captured by the dominant axes is \emph{conditional} on whether
those axes are actually varied in the experiment being audited.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Iter-197 paired-bootstrap ratio CIs (B=2000, seed 20260706, 95\%).
Each row reports the ratio $\eta^2_\text{stack} / \eta^2_\text{algo}$ for one
(channel $\times$ stack axis) cell. ``CI excludes 1'' means the
lower-bound of the ratio CI is strictly greater than 1; ``CI excludes 3''
means the lower-bound is strictly greater than 3.}
\label{tab:p5-iter197-ratio-robustness}
\begin{tabular}{l l r r r r}
\hline
channel & stack axis & point & CI lo & CI hi & excl.\,1 \\
\hline
\multicolumn{6}{l}{\emph{dominant axes --- signal present}} \\
zvf    & task\_slice   & 10.32$\times$ &  3.33 & 52.61 & \checkmark \\
zvf    & $G$            &  9.77$\times$ &  2.91 & 43.52 & \checkmark \\
reward & model\_family  & 60.63$\times$ &  5.62 & 247.88 & \checkmark \\
reward & task\_slice   & 36.55$\times$ &  3.52 & 184.14 & \checkmark \\
reward & $G$            &  4.08$\times$ &  0.37 & 40.95 & --- \\
len    & model\_family  &  4.76$\times$ &  1.51 & 16.72 & \checkmark \\
len    & task\_slice   &  3.32$\times$ &  0.79 & 12.27 & --- \\
len    & temperature   &  3.27$\times$ &  0.81 & 11.78 & --- \\
\hline
\multicolumn{6}{l}{\emph{noisy axes --- $\eta^2 \approx 0$, CI includes 1}} \\
zvf    & model\_family  &  0.12$\times$ &  0.00 &  2.42 & --- \\
zvf    & temperature    &  0.20$\times$ &  0.00 &  2.79 & --- \\
zvf    & seed           &  0.00$\times$ &  0.00 &  1.68 & --- \\
reward & temperature    &  0.00$\times$ &  0.00 &  5.89 & --- \\
reward & seed           &  0.00$\times$ &  0.00 &  7.30 & --- \\
len    & $G$            &  0.27$\times$ &  0.08 &  3.93 & --- \\
len    & seed           &  0.00$\times$ &  0.00 &  0.95 & --- \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{Cross-coupling.}
Exhibit~21 closes iter-193's two open weaknesses (axis-selection bias,
ratio CI). It couples to (i) Exhibit~9 (algorithm-axis $\eta^2 \le 6.3\%$)
--- the algorithm axis is the LEAST informative, regardless of which
non-trivial stack axis is the comparator; (ii) Exhibit~7 (stack-axis
$\eta^2$) --- the dominant axes (task / model / $G$) explain $45$--$47\%$
of variance, but this is conditional on the experimental design varying
those axes; (iii) iter-141 (algorithm axis alone) --- $\eta^2_\text{algo} \approx 0$ survives every stress test; (iv) the \emph{Frontier
Round 1} Estimator-Equivalence Principle --- the algorithm-vs-dominant-stack
ratio's $10$--$60\times$ magnitude is the sharpest quantitative form of
the principle: the label is a near-zero predictor of outcomes once the
stack is fixed (or, here, once the dominant stack axes are varied).

\textbf{Operational.} (a) \textbf{REFINE} iter-193's headline: report
ratios as ``$10$--$60\times$ on the DOMINANT stack axes
(\texttt{task\_slice} / \texttt{model\_family} / $G$); degenerate on
the NOISY axes (\texttt{seed} / \texttt{temperature}) where the
experimental design does not vary the axis.'' (b) \textbf{WIRE} the
3-dominant-axis composite as a CI gate: any new corpus should report
$\eta^2_{\text{composite},\text{dom3}}$ and the corresponding ratio CI;
fail if the composite CI includes $1$ on the diagnostic outcome
(\textsc{ZVF}). (c) \textbf{EXTEND} in next iter to per-axis stratified
ratio: does the dominance survive stratification by \texttt{task\_slice}
(does the model vs $G$ ordering hold within each task)?

\textbf{Reproduction.}
\texttt{python3 scripts/p5p8/p5\_iter197\_ratio\_sensitivity.py}
($\sim$60s, stdlib only). Outputs:
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_iter197\_paired\_boot.tsv}
(15 rows: 3 channels $\times$ 5 axes),
\texttt{p5\_iter197\_worst\_axis\_stress.tsv} (3 rows),
\texttt{p5\_iter197\_jackknife\_axis.tsv} (15 rows),
\texttt{p5\_iter197\_composite\_5axis.tsv} (3 rows),
\texttt{p5\_iter197\_composite\_dominant3.tsv} (3 rows),
\texttt{p5\_iter197\_summary.json}.
 \section{The Eight-Item Minimum Reportable Stack}
\label{sec:p5-stack}
A reporting standard survives only if it is short, checkable, and every item is
load-bearing. We therefore admit an item to the minimum reportable stack only
if we can point to a \emph{documented lever}: evidence that varying the item,
with the rest held fixed, moved or flipped a reported outcome or its
telemetry. \tableref{tab:p5-minreport} is the standard; the subsections justify
each row. Items 1--3 specify what objective was optimized and on what
substrate; items 4--5 specify the signal geometry of group-relative training;
items 6--8 specify what the headline number can legitimately claim.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{The eight-item minimum reportable stack. Each item earns its place
via a documented lever---evidence that the item alone can move or flip a
reported result. Provenance strata as defined in \secref{sec:p5-results}.}
\label{tab:p5-minreport}
\footnotesize
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{@{}cp{2.4cm}p{4.4cm}p{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\# & Item & What must be reported & Documented lever \\
\midrule
1 & Loss form & Ratio level (token/sequence), clip values and asymmetry,
    token mask, advantage normalization, dynamic-sampling status &
    Same label, opposite ZVF telemetry across loss forms
    (0.00 vs.\ 0.58, \secref{sec:p5-exhibit-label}); normalization biases
    \citep{tong2025drgrpo} \\
2 & Reference policy \& KL & Reference snapshot, KL coefficient and estimator,
    or explicit ``no KL'' &
    Distinguishes GRPO-family members that are otherwise conflated
    \citep{shao2024deepseekmath, yu2025dapo}; silently alters the objective \\
3 & Sampler / backend / precision & Training backend, sampling engine,
    precision, decoding parameters &
    $17\times$ same-label outcome flip from a nominal backend comparison
    that also bundled a different base checkpoint
    (\secref{sec:p5-exhibit-backend}) \\
4 & Per-step ZVF/GU trajectory & Per-step zero-variance fraction and gradient
    utilization $\mathrm{GU} = 1-\mathrm{ZVF}$ &
    ZVF is U-shaped in accuracy (\tableref{tab:p5-ushape}): a mean hides
    whether signal starved from mastery or incapacity \\
5 & Group-size schedule & $G$ per step, including adaptive escalation rules &
    ZVF falls systematically with $G$ (\tableref{tab:p5-gsize}); theory:
    $\E[\mathrm{ZVF}] = p^G + (1-p)^G$ at per-prompt accuracy $p$ \\
6 & Held-out split & Split identity, size, and disjointness from the reward
    environment &
    Train reward and held-out accuracy dissociate; reward-side
    overoptimization is well documented \citep{gao2023reward} \\
7 & Decontamination \& parser probe & Contamination check performed;
    sensitivity of reward/telemetry to parser perturbation &
    Benchmark contamination shifts headline scores \citep{zhang2024gsm1k,
    riddell2024contamination}; micro-jitter $\epsilon \sim U(0,10^{-4})$
    collapses batch ZVF $0.158 \to 0.000$ \\
8 & Pass@$k$ curves & Held-out pass@$k$ at $k \in \{1, 8, 32\}$ (or stated
    subset), with sampling temperature and completion budget &
    Matched base-vs-post-RL panel: pass@1 deltas scatter $-0.5$ to $+1.9$
    points (the $G{=}2$ arm reads as a regression) while the pass@32
    frontier improves $+1.5$ to $+2.5$ points in all four arms \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

\subsection{Item 1: Loss Form}
\label{sec:p5-item1}
The label is not the loss. ``DAPO'' with and without dynamic sampling are
different optimizers with opposite ZVF signatures
(\secref{sec:p5-exhibit-label}); Dr.GRPO exists because GRPO's
std-normalization and length terms bias the update \citep{tong2025drgrpo};
GSPO moves the importance ratio from token to sequence level
\citep{qwen2025gspo}; and the group-relative loss family is close enough to
preference optimization that GRPO admits a DPO reading
\citep{rafailov2024direct, wu2025grpo_dpo}, making the exact contrast structure
part of the objective's identity. The reportable unit is therefore the tuple
(ratio level, clip low/high, token mask, advantage normalization,
dynamic-sampling status), not the name.

\subsection{Item 2: Reference Policy and KL Handling}
\label{sec:p5-item2}
Whether a run regularizes toward a frozen reference, a moving reference, or
nothing at all changes what is being optimized; GRPO as introduced carries a
KL term \citep{shao2024deepseekmath} that many descendants drop or re-estimate
differently. Because the KL term interacts with the clip range in determining
how far the policy can move per step, two runs labeled identically but
differing in KL handling can occupy different stability regimes. This item is
cheap to report (three fields) and its omission is unrecoverable post hoc.

\subsection{Item 3: Sampler, Backend, and Precision}
\label{sec:p5-item3}
Exhibit 1 is the lever: a nominal backend comparison produced the largest
same-label swing in our corpus ($5.0\% \to 85.6\%$), but provenance inspection
also found a different base checkpoint. The number is thus evidence that item~3
must record both backend and checkpoint identity, not evidence that the backend
alone caused the gap. Serving engines differ in kernel
selection, batching, and numerics \citep{kwon2023vllm, zheng2024sglang,
dao2022flashattention}, and in on-policy RL these differences compound: the
sampler's distribution \emph{is} the data distribution. A closed backend is
not disqualifying---but it must be named, versioned where possible, and its
precision and decoding parameters stated, so that a reader knows the result is
conditioned on it; the exact base checkpoint and revision must be reported in
the same record.

\subsection{Item 4: The Per-Step ZVF/GU Trajectory}
\label{sec:p5-item4}
A single summary number cannot substitute for the trajectory, because ZVF
aliases opposite regimes. Across a 368-run audit (Stratum B; drawn from a
790-run corpus), mean ZVF is U-shaped in accuracy (\tableref{tab:p5-ushape}):
near-zero-reward runs and near-saturated runs both show ZVF near $0.9$--$1.0$,
while mid-accuracy runs sit near $0.25$. Theory makes the aliasing exact: with
per-prompt accuracy $p$ and binary rewards, the expected zero-variance
indicator is $p^G + (1-p)^G$, symmetric in $p \leftrightarrow 1-p$, and the
usable signal scales as $p(1-p)$ (validated directionally at toy scale,
Stratum C: corr(grad\_norm, $p(1-p)$) $= +0.71$ on a 0.5B model with an open
backward pass---an effect direction, not a publishable effect size). Reporting
the per-step trajectory (and $\mathrm{GU} = 1 - \mathrm{ZVF}$) lets readers
see \emph{which} arm of the U a run occupied; the companion Pillar-2 paper
develops the diagnostic and its failure modes in full.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering

its accuracy regime.}
\label{tab:p5-ushape}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}

\midrule

\subsection{Item 5: Group-Size Schedule}
\label{sec:p5-item5}
Group size is a signal-geometry knob, not a throughput knob: larger $G$ lowers
the probability that a group is degenerate (from the same expression
$p^G + (1-p)^G$), and the effect is large in practice
(\tableref{tab:p5-gsize}). Because adaptive schedules now exist---our
open-trainer audit escalated $G$ from 4 to 6 to 8 live on ZVF spikes
(\secref{sec:p5-toolchain})---reporting a single scalar $G$ is no longer
sufficient; the schedule is part of the optimizer. The companion Pillar-3
paper of \ours{} treats group size in depth, including the rollout-budget
trade-off highlighted by 2-GRPO-style analyses \citep{wu2025grpo_dpo,
tan2025scalingrl}.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering

June 2026; ``--'' = not run). Larger $G$ lowers ZVF where accuracy is
intermediate, as theory predicts; near the extremes of the U-shape the effect
compresses.}
\label{tab:p5-gsize}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
Model & $G=4$ & $G=8$ & $G=16$ \\
\midrule

Qwen3-4B-Instruct & 0.89 & 0.84 & 0.91 \\
Llama-3.1-8B      & 0.76 & 0.65 & 0.47 \\

\subsection{Item 6: A Held-Out Split Disjoint from the Reward Environment}
\label{sec:p5-item6}
Training reward is measured inside the same apparatus that generates the
learning signal, so it inherits every quirk of that apparatus---parser
leniency, template artifacts, reward misspecification
\citep{gao2023reward, krakovna2020specification}. In the \ours{} group-size
sweep, train-side telemetry moved substantially while held-out accuracy was
flat, a dissociation that would be invisible without a disjoint split. The
requirement is deliberately minimal: name the split, state its size, and
certify it is disjoint from the prompts and the verifier loop used for
training.

\subsection{Item 7: Decontamination and a Parser-Robustness Probe}
\label{sec:p5-item7}
Contamination inflates headline numbers \citep{zhang2024gsm1k,
riddell2024contamination}; that much is standard. The less familiar half of
this item is the parser probe: because rewards are computed by string-level
verifiers, sub-reward perturbations can flip telemetry wholesale. In our
convergent repository experiment, adding micro-jitter
$\epsilon \sim U(0, 10^{-4})$ to rewards collapsed batch ZVF from $0.158$ to
$0.000$ while a magnitude-aware alternative (pairwise contrast density,
$\mathrm{PCD} = \frac{G-1}{G}\,\E[p(1-p)]$) moved by $3\times 10^{-7}$. A
diagnostic that a parser's rounding behavior can zero out is a diagnostic
whose reported value is a stack property. The probe we ask for is one line:
perturb rewards below the verifier's resolution and report which telemetry
survives.

\subsection{Item 8: Pass@$k$ Curves Alongside Pass@1}
\label{sec:p5-item8}
Pass@1 conflates two different improvements: sharpening the output
distribution over solutions the base model could already reach, and
expanding the set of reachable solutions. Roughly two-thirds of
RL-for-reasoning papers report only pass@1, while studies that report
pass@$k$ frontiers repeatedly find base models matching or overtaking
their RL-tuned counterparts at large $k$ --- i.e., the ``gain'' was
distribution sharpening. Because group-relative training acts directly on
the sampling distribution, this is the \emph{expected} failure mode for
the GRPO family, not an exotic one. The documented lever: on a matched
base-vs-post-RL panel (four adapters at $G\in\{2,4,16,32\}$, one stack,
same problems), pass@1 deltas scatter from $-0.5$ to $+1.9$ points ---
under pass@1-only reporting the $G{=}2$ arm would be called a regression
--- while the pass@32 frontier tells the opposite, consistent story: all
four arms improve by $+1.5$ to $+2.5$ points. Neither reading reaches
significance at a single training seed, which is itself the point:
without pass@$k$ curves and stated noise bands, the panel supports any
narrative a reader prefers. \emph{Report:} held-out pass@$k$ at
$k \in \{1, 8, 32\}$ (or a stated subset with justification), with the
sampling temperature and completion budget used for the estimate.

\subsection{Live-Corpus Coupling: How the MIN-REPORT Fingerprint Relates to Measured Telemetry}
\label{sec:p5-live-coupling}
The first seven items above form the seven-field run-manifest fingerprint used in this
coupling analysis (item 8 is an evaluation-report requirement rather than
a manifest field, and postdates the corpus below); the question a
reviewer will ask is whether the resulting manifest \emph{actually predicts}
the measured telemetry on a live corpus. We test this on the
98-cell mega-campaign manifest corpus
(\texttt{experiments/results/mega\_20260704/manifests/}, paired with
\texttt{cells.tsv}; analysed by
\texttt{scripts/p5p8/p5\_manifest\_outcome\_coupling.py}, see
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_manifest\_outcome\_coupling\.*}). On
the live corpus, the seven-field manifest fingerprint carries a total of
$11.4$ bits of cross-cell information; four of the seven fields
(\textit{loss\_form}, \textit{ref\_policy\_kl},
\textit{sampler\_backend\_precision}, and the per-cell
\textit{per\_step\_zvf\_path}, which is unique-per-cell and thus
behaves as a cell identifier rather than a stack descriptor) contribute
exactly $0$ bits, while the remaining three stack-descriptor items
(\textit{group\_size\_schedule}, \textit{heldout\_split},
\textit{decontamination\_notes}) carry all $11.4$ bits. The badge is
therefore constant across cells and cannot discriminate seed-pair
reward dispersion within a cell-group (Spearman $\rho = +0.000$, CI
$[+0.000, +0.000]$, $n=48$ pairs); but the manifest \emph{fingerprint as a
whole} is a strong predictor of joint measured telemetry. Across
$2000$ sampled cell-pairs, the Hamming distance on the seven-field manifest
predicts the absolute difference on ZVF (Spearman $\rho = +0.529$,
close-vs-far permutation $p \le 10^{-3}$), on PCD
($\rho = +0.541$, $p \le 10^{-3}$), and on mean-reward
($\rho = +0.251$, $p = 0.003$). The pattern is concentrated entirely in
the three items that carry cross-cell information: per-item permutation
max-$|\Delta\text{mean}|$ tests return $p \le 10^{-3}$ for
\textit{heldout\_split} and \textit{decontamination\_notes} on all three
channels and for \textit{group\_size\_schedule} on ZVF/PCD, while the
four placebo items return NaN (no test possible) or $p = 1.000$. The
operational reading: a manifest-emit pipeline that varies only on
operational metadata makes MIN-REPORT \emph{auditable} but not
\emph{discriminative} on the algorithm axis; the standard's predictive
power is bounded by what the emitters actually vary on. The sharp
operational recommendation that falls out of this measurement is
additive: tag each emit with the set of stack axes it varies on, so
downstream consumers can automatically report the axis-discrimination
ceiling alongside the badge.

\subsection{The Placebo-Replacement Bound: Why the Schema Is Not the Constraint}
\label{sec:p5-placebo-bound}
The 4-of-7 placebo pattern could be read two ways: (A) the schema picks
the wrong items and a redesigned v2 schema would escape the placebo
problem; or (B) any stack-descriptor schema would be a placebo on a
single-stack corpus, and the binding constraint is the experimental
design. We test (A) on the live corpus by projecting six v2 candidates
(analysed by
\texttt{scripts/p5p8/p5\_placebo\_replacement\_analysis.py}, see
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_placebo\_replacement\.*}). Of the
six candidates, four are GRPO/PPO hyperparameters from the canonical
RL-for-LLM literature --- \textit{kl\_coefficient}
\citep{schulman2017proximal, lambert2024tulu3}, \textit{clip\_range\_low}
\citep{schulman2017proximal}, \textit{advantage\_normalization}
\citep{shao2024deepseekmath, yu2025dapo}, and
\textit{mini\_batch\_size} \citep{ivison2024unpacking, lambert2024tulu3}.
All four project to \emph{n\_unique}=1 on the live 98-cell corpus
because the corpus is a single-stack Tinker-closed campaign that does
not vary these axes; the projected info-budget uplift is exactly
$\Delta\text{bits} = 0$ with a $95\%$ bootstrap CI (B=2000,
seed=20260705) that excludes any positive gain. Adding all four to the
v2 schema would replicate the iter-65 4-placebo problem at higher
cardinality without escaping it. The remaining two candidates are
corpus-controlled: \textit{temperature\_schedule} (cells.tsv varies
$T \in \{0.6, 1.0\}$; H\_bits $= 0.995$ on the live corpus) would add
one varying item to the v2 schema and lift total info budget from
$11.41$ to $12.41$ bits; \textit{model\_family\_label} is redundant
because it is already a column of cells.tsv (H\_bits $= 0.999$ but
net-new info $= 0$). The $\eta^2$ variance partition at the
manifest-vs-cells.tsv granularity (Berkeley row
\textit{unpacking\_dpo\_ppo\_factorization} recipe replayed on this
corpus) confirms the reading: stack axes ($G$, task\_slice,
temperature, model\_family) explain $94.7\%$ of ZVF variance on the
$98$ cells ($\eta^2_{\text{stack}} = 0.947$, $25$ unique buckets),
while $\eta^2_{\text{algo}} = 0$ by construction (single-algorithm
campaign). The remaining $5.3\%$ is seed-level residual. Reading (A)
is therefore refuted: the schema is not the binding constraint, the
corpus is. The sharp operational recommendation: add
\textit{temperature\_schedule} to the v2 MIN-REPORT schema and
document the four GRPO/PPO hyperparameter items as
\textit{deferred-to-cross-stack-campaign} until a v2 mega-campaign
varies the relevant axes.
 \section{Cross-Corpus Portability of the Seven-Field MIN-REPORT Run Manifest}
\label{sec:p5-cross-corpus}

The seven-field run-manifest portion of the MIN-REPORT standard in \secref{sec:p5-stack} was
validated on a single corpus (the 98-cell mega campaign of
\secref{sec:p5-exhibit-coverage}). A remaining question is whether
the standard \emph{generalizes} across the heterogeneous experiment
corpora in this repository---or whether it is a schema custom-built
for one campaign. We answer with a portability test
(\texttt{scripts/p5p8/p5\_cross\_corpus\_portability.py};
\texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_cross\_corpus\_portability.*}).

\paragraph{Method.}
Apply the seven MIN-REPORT run-manifest fields to seven internal corpora and
measure, for each, (a) per-item coverage, (b) per-item variance,
(c) total bits, (d) mean Hamming discrimination across random record
pairs (B=2000 bootstrap, seed 20260705), and (e) a STRONG /
PORTABLE / LIMITED / NULL verdict. The seven corpora are
deliberately heterogeneous: the full 98-cell manifest (C1); the
N2 four-method same-stack tensors (C2); the N10 2-algo $\times$
8-seed expansion manifest (C3); and four rows-only summary corpora
(base-instruct paired, group-size paired, length-bias paired, ZVF
per-stratum AUROC).

\paragraph{Verdict rule.}
STRONG iff $\geq 5$ of 7 manifest fields populate \emph{and} $\geq 1$ carries
variance; PORTABLE iff $\geq 3$ populate \emph{and} $\geq 1$
varies; LIMITED iff $\geq 1$ populates \emph{and} $\geq 1$ varies;
NULL otherwise.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{Cross-corpus portability of the seven-field MIN-REPORT run manifest
fingerprint. $n$ = number of manifest records; pop = number of
items populated; var = number of items with $\geq 2$ unique values;
$H$ = mean Hamming distance across bootstrap pairs with 95\% CI.}
\label{tab:p5-cross-corpus}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
\toprule
Corpus & $n$ & pop & var & total\_bits & $H$ & verdict \\
\midrule
C1 mega\_20260704            &  98 & 7/7 & 3/7 & 4.80 & 1.891 [1.858, 2.028] & \textbf{STRONG} \\
C2 n2\_reward\_tensor         &   4 & 7/7 & 1/7 & 2.00 & 1.000 [1.000, 1.000] & \textbf{STRONG} \\
C3 n10\_seed\_expansion       &  16 & 7/7 & 2/7 & 4.00 & 1.716 [1.690, 1.810] & \textbf{STRONG} \\
C4 base\_instruct\_paired     &   8 & 2/7 & 1/7 & 0.95 & 0.555 [0.518, 0.606] & \textbf{LIMITED} \\
C5 group\_size\_iter111       &   4 & 4/7 & 1/7 & 2.00 & 1.000 [1.000, 1.000] & \textbf{PORTABLE} \\
C6 length\_bias\_iter60       &  20 & 2/7 & 2/7 & 2.52 & 1.213 [1.150, 1.260] & \textbf{LIMITED} \\
C7 zvf\_iter118\_auroc        &   8 & 3/7 & 2/7 & 3.00 & 1.472 [1.418, 1.502] & \textbf{PORTABLE} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Headline.}
Across the seven corpora, mean \texttt{n\_items\_populated} =
\textbf{4.57/7} with 95\% bootstrap CI [3.00, 6.29], confirming that
the standard is portable but stratified. The verdict distribution is
3 STRONG, 2 PORTABLE, 2 LIMITED, 0 NULL---no corpus is a complete
schema-failure. Per-item coverage across corpora reveals a sharp
separation: Items 1 (\textit{loss\_form}) and 6
(\textit{heldout\_split}) are populated in \emph{every} corpus
(7/7); Items 2, 3, 7 (KL, backend, decontam) are populated only in
manifest-level corpora (C1/C2/C3); Item 4 (\textit{zvf\_gu\_trajectory})
is populated in 5/7; Item 5 (\textit{group\_size\_schedule}) in 4/7.

\paragraph{Cross-paper coupling.}
This iter independently reproduces the iter-65 placebo pattern from
the fingerprint-as-data path: on C1 four items carry \texttt{n\_unique}=1
because the campaign is a single-stack Tinker-closed run, while the
other three (group\_size\_schedule, heldout\_split,
decontamination\_notes) carry all 4.80 bits of cross-cell information.
The iter-69 placebo-replacement finding is confirmed at a higher
abstraction: the same schema yields STRONG on C1 and LIMITED on C4/C6
even though loaders faithfully apply every item---the variance-deficit
is a corpus property, not a schema property. The iter-73 v2.0
stack-axis extension is sharpened: the +60.1\% info-budget uplift is
informative on C1-class multi-stack campaigns but yields 0 uplift on
same-stack corpora (C2 N2 $G{=}8$ fixed).

\paragraph{Operational recommendation.}
For rows-only summary corpora (C4--C7), MIN-REPORT v3.0 should declare
Items 2/3/7 as \textit{honest-n/a} (``n/a-rows-only'') rather than
fail the audit; this single change lifts all 7 corpora to PORTABLE
without altering the schema.
 \subsection{Yield-aware MIN-REPORT v2.1 axis (Item 13: \texttt{zvf\_yield\_residual})}
\label{sec:p5-iter80-yield}

The iter-66 row 77 \texttt{measured\_yield\_residual} block argued that the
per-step anti-herding bonus
$\delta_{\mathrm{div}} = \mathrm{ZVF}_{\mathrm{obs}} - \mathrm{ZVF}_{\mathrm{iid}}$ is the
one signal that varies per-method on the same-stack N2 corpus. The
iter-72 primary recommendation proposed extending MIN-REPORT with a
per-cell $\delta_{\mathrm{div}}$ item to replace one of the iter-65
``placebo'' items with a measurable yield-residual axis.

We instantiate this proposal as Item 13 of a v2.1 MIN-REPORT:
\begin{align}
  \mathrm{Item13}(c) \;=\; z_{\mathrm{obs}}(c) \;-\; \bigl(p_c^{\,G_c} + (1-p_c)^{\,G_c}\bigr),
\quad p_c \;=\; \mathrm{mean\_reward}(c)/100.
\label{eq:item13}
\end{align}

\subsubsection{Information-budget uplift (H1)}
On the live 98-cell corpus, Item~13 populates $\mathbf{98/98}$ cells (the
formula is fully determined by $(G_c, \mathrm{mean\_reward}, \mathrm{zvf})$,
all already in \texttt{cells.tsv}). Discrete-cardinality:
$n_{\mathrm{unique}} = 58$ (at $10^{-4}$ rounding precision) -- Item~13 is
\emph{much more} discriminating than any of the 7 v1 items. Shannon
information added to the v2.0 fingerprint:
$\Delta H \;=\; +4.645\,\mathrm{bits}$, bootstrap CI $[+3.66, +4.65]$.

v1 = 11.41 bits; v2.0 adds 5 stack axes = +6.86 bits; v2.1 adds Item 13
(zvf\_yield\_residual) = +4.65 bits. Item 13 contributes 67.7\% as much
information as the entire 5-axis stack extension.}
\label{tab:p5-v21-uplift}
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}

Fingerprint & Items & Pop. cells & $H_{\mathrm{bits}}$ total & $\Delta H$ vs v1 \\
\midrule
v1 (paper §4.2)        & 7 & 98/98 & 11.41 & -- \\

\subsubsection{Spearman $\rho$(Hamming, $|\Delta z|)$ uplift (H2)}
On 2000 sampled cell-pairs from the live corpus:
\begin{itemize}
  \item v1 fingerprint alone: $\rho = 0.4268$
  \item v2.0 (v1 + 5 stack axes): $\rho = 0.3835$ -- the v2.0 stack axes
        \emph{weaken} coupling by $-0.043$ (consistent with iter-73 row 86 H2).
  \item v2.1 (v2.0 + Item 13): $\rho = 0.4384$ -- Item 13 \emph{restores} and
        \emph{strengthens} coupling by $+0.055$ vs v2.0 (paired bootstrap CI
        $[+0.050,\,+0.062]$, \textbf{excludes zero}).
\end{itemize}
This is the \textbf{first MIN-REPORT item that independently strengthens
fingerprint-vs-outcome coupling on the live 98-cell corpus} -- a sharp
contrast to iter-65 row 76 (4/7 v1 items are placebo) and iter-73 row 86
(v2 stack axes weaken coupling by 0.043).

\subsubsection{Per-cell badge uplift (H3)}
Deterministic: each cell's badge rises by exactly 20 points (the proposed
weight of Item 13). The 95\% bootstrap CI on per-cell uplift is degenerate.

\subsubsection{Per-axis Spearman (H4)}
Item 13 alone vs $|\Delta z|$ gives $\rho = 0.366$ on 2000 cell-pairs --
already 86\% of the v1 fingerprint's $\rho = 0.427$, despite being a
single continuous axis. This is a sharper single-axis signal than any
v2 stack axis at the 1-D Hamming granularity.

\subsubsection{Verdict and operational recommendation}
\textit{SIGNAL-BEARING.} Item 13 contributes both
(i) high information content ($+4.65$ bits) and
(ii) measurable outcome-coupling strengthening (paired $\Delta\rho$ excludes zero).
We recommend Item 13 be adopted into a v2.1 MIN-REPORT schema, displacing
\textbf{one} v1 placebo item. The iter-69 row 81 finding (``placebo is
corpus-design, not schema-design'') is sharpened: at least one
yield-residual axis is empirically signal-bearing across every stack-pair
configuration on the live 98-cell corpus.

\subsubsection{Cross-paper coupling}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{P6 iter-66 row 77 / iter-70 row 82 / iter-74 row 87} --
        Item 13 is the row-level summarization of the registry's
        \texttt{measured\_yield\_residual} $\delta_{\mathrm{div}}$ block.
        The per-cell instantiation makes the registry axis reproducible
        across any corpus with the $(G, p, z)$ columns.
  \item \textbf{P7 iter-72 row 85 / iter-75 row 88 / iter-79 row 93} --
        Item 13 captures the per-(method, step) contrast-preservation bonus
        at the cell-summary granularity; together with iter-75's exact
        hypergeometric \texttt{CP\_exact}, the structural anti-herding signal
        is now end-to-end machine-readable from cells.tsv to the joint
        controller's per-step savings.
  \item \textbf{P8 iter-76 row 89 / iter-80 row 94} -- the same
        anti-herding bonus that makes Item 13 signal-bearing on the LLM-RL
        axis also explains why score-stream gradient (P8 row 94) is more
        LLM-efficient than absolute-band: rows where consecutive scores
        plateau are exactly the rows where cheap-vs-expensive disagreement
        dominates -- a per-row analog of the per-cell Item 13.
\end{itemize}
 \subsection{Multi-axis yield-residual v2.2 (Items 14--17)}
\label{sec:p5-iter81-yield-axes}

The iter-80 row 95 Item~13 (\texttt{zvf\_yield\_residual}) was the \emph{first}
MIN-REPORT item to independently strengthen fingerprint-vs-outcome coupling.
The iter-80 primary recommendation asked whether MIN-REPORT is structurally
under-recorded by a single item, or whether \emph{multiple} yield-residual
axes each carry independent signal. We close that recommendation here by
testing four additional yield-residual candidates per cell, all derived
from the live \texttt{group\_tensors/} data already in the repository.

\subsubsection{Four candidates (Items 14--17)}
Each candidate is a per-cell scalar fully determined by the
$\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{vec}} \in \{0,\dots,G\}^{\,n_{\mathrm{groups}}}$ vector of
success-counts across prompts and the per-cell $p_c = \mathrm{mean\_reward}/100$:
\begin{align}
  \mathrm{Item14}(c) &\;=\; \mathrm{Var}(K) - G \cdot p \cdot (1-p) \tag{K-variance residual}\\
  \mathrm{Item15}(c) &\;=\; |\{\,k : k \in K\,\}| \tag{distinct K-count, $\le G{+}1$}\\
  \mathrm{Item16}(c) &\;=\; \max_k\,\bigl(\#\{x : K_x = k\}\bigr) / n_{\mathrm{groups}} \tag{max K-share}\\
  \mathrm{Item17}(c) &\;=\; \mathrm{Var}\!\bigl(K/G\bigr) \tag{prompt-$\hat p$ spread}\\
  \label{eq:items14-17}
\end{align}
All four are populated on $\mathbf{98/98}$ cells with zero additional harvest.

\subsubsection{Information budget uplift (H1)}
Table~\ref{tab:p5-v22-uplift} shows the cumulative fingerprint budget.
Items~14--17 collectively add $+15.86$ bits to v2.1, $\mathbf{3.4\times}$
as much as Item~13 alone. The total v2.2 budget is $38.78$ bits
($+69.2\%$ over v2.1; $+147.6\%$ over v1). Per-item cardinality on the
98-cell corpus: Item 14 $n_{\mathrm{unique}} = 49$, Item 15 $= 5$ (bounded
by $G+1$), Item 16 $= 20$, Item 17 $= 49$.

a placebo} (excess H bits = $-0.123$, $\approx -1.9\sigma$).}

\subsubsection{Single-item Spearman (H2)}
\textbf{Each individual item 14--17 alone} has higher fingerprint-vs-$|\Delta z|$
Spearman $\rho$ than the \emph{full seven-field v1 fingerprint}:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Item 14 (K-variance residual): $\rho = 0.558$
  \item Item 15 (K-unique count): $\rho = 0.588$
  \item Item 16 (max K-share): $\rho = 0.589$
  \item Item 17 (prompt-$\hat p$ spread): $\rho = 0.556$
\end{itemize}
The v1 fingerprint's $\rho = 0.436$ is exceeded by $\sim 30\%$ even at
single-axis granularity -- evidence that the structural \emph{shape} of
the success-count distribution carries more anti-herding signal than
any string-valued fingerprint axis.

\subsubsection{Paired bootstrap coupling (H3)}
Paired bootstrap ($B=2000$ with $N_{\mathrm{pairs}}=2000$ cell-pairs):
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\Delta\rho(\mathrm{v2.2}_{\mathrm{all}} - \mathrm{v2.1}) = \mathbf{+0.0748}$, 95\% CI $[\mathbf{+0.066,\,+0.084}]$ -- excludes zero.
  \item $\Delta\rho(\mathrm{v2.2}_{13+15} - \mathrm{v2.1}) = +0.0443$ (CI $[+0.039,\,+0.049]$) -- the strongest \emph{single} candidate.
  \item Items 14, 16, 17 each add $+0.031$ to $+0.039$ with CIs excluding zero.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Every} (Item 13 + one new item) combination independently strengthens
fingerprint-vs-outcome coupling.

\subsubsection{Binomial null control (H4)}
To separate signal from binomial noise we simulated per-cell
$K_x \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(G_c,\, p_c)$ for $n=200$ replications and
recomputed each item's $H_{\mathrm{bits}}$. Excess
$\Delta H_{\mathrm{bits}} = H_{\mathrm{obs}} - \bar H_{\mathrm{null}}$:
\begin{align}
  \mathrm{Item14:}\;&\ \Delta H = +0.186\quad(\!\approx\!+2.8\sigma), &
  \mathrm{Item15:}\;&\ \Delta H = +0.255\quad(\!\approx\!+6.2\sigma), \\
  \mathrm{Item16:}\;&\ \Delta H = -0.123\quad(\!\approx\!-1.9\sigma), &
  \mathrm{Item17:}\;&\ \Delta H = +0.257\quad(\!\approx\!+3.6\sigma)
  \label{eq:null_excess}
\end{align}
\textbf{Items 14, 15, 17} encode information \emph{beyond} $\mathrm{Binomial}(G,p)$
they are signal-bearing. \textbf{Item 16 is REJECTED}: its empirical
max-K-share is \emph{higher} than binomial expectation (more concentrated),
i.e. it is anti-herding-by-construction but does not add new empirical
information beyond what the binomial model's K-distribution already encodes.

\subsubsection{Verdict and operational recommendation}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Adopt Items 14, 15, 17} into MIN-REPORT v2.2.
  \item \textbf{Reject Item 16} (max K-share) -- negative binomial-null excess.
  \item \textbf{Displace} one v1 placebo item (e.g.\ \texttt{sampler\_backend\_precision}, $n_{\mathrm{unique}}=1$ on the live corpus).
  \item Recommended \textbf{v2.2 schema = 14 items}, $H_{\mathrm{bits}}$ budget $= 35.20$, paired $\Delta\rho$ CI excludes zero.
\end{itemize}
The frontier synthesis (FRONTIER\_INSIGHTS.md Round 1) frame
Y$(p,G) = 1 - \mathrm{ZVF}$ as \emph{Contrastive Yield}; iter-80 Item~13 is
its first per-cell realisation, and iter-81 items 14, 15, 17 add
\textbf{three} further realisations of contrast-yield information at the
$(K$-distribution) granularity. The four v2.2 items together give a
\textbf{4-vector of yield-residual signal} per cell, sufficient to
reconstruct the underlying GRPO contrast-preservation regime.

\subsubsection{Cross-paper coupling}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{P5 iter-80 row 95 Item 13} -- Items 14, 15, 17 sharpen the
        single-axis signal into a 4-vector of yield-residual signal; Item 16
        rejected explicitly falsifies the iter-80 mint's `add ALL items' reading.
  \item \textbf{P6 iter-66 row 77 / iter-78 row 92} -- Items 14-17 extend
        the registry's \texttt{measured\_yield\_residual} block from a single
        $\delta_{\mathrm{div}}$ scalar to a 4-vector at zero harvest cost.
  \item \textbf{P7 iter-75 row 88 / iter-79 row 93} -- the joint controller's
        per-step ZVF-triage is the \emph{per-step} analog of Items 14-17 here;
        iter-75's \texttt{CP\_exact} formula and iter-79's per-seed CV measure
        the same anti-herding preservation at finer temporal granularity.
  \item \textbf{P8 iter-80 row 94 (score-stream gradient)} -- the
        $s_{i-1} - s_i$ gradient on fraud rows is the \emph{per-row anti-herding}
        analog of the per-cell Items 14-17 here. Same mechanism, two
        granularities, two operational wins (LLM-call savings + fingerprint
        bits).
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Reproducibility}
\begin{itemize}
  \item Script: \texttt{scripts/p5p8/p5\_yield\_residual\_axes.py} ($\sim$290 LoC, stdlib + numpy).
  \item Outputs: \texttt{experiments/results/p5p8/p5\_yield\_residual\_axes\{.tsv,\_per\_item.tsv,\_shuffle\_null.tsv,\_summary.json\}}.
  \item Seed: 20260705 (paired bootstrap $B=2000$ with $N_{\mathrm{pairs}}=2000$; binomial null $n=200$).
  \item Citations: frontier synthesis (Round 1: \emph{Contrastive Yield} Y$(p,G)=1-\mathrm{ZVF}$); iter-80 row 95 Item 13 baseline; iter-77 row 91 portability framework.
\end{itemize}
 \subsection{Ivison 2024 unpacking of the N2 four-method same-stack panel (iter 85)}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-ivison-unpacking}

The brief's vein (b) asks: \emph{quantify stack-conditioning with the N2
four-method same-stack tensors and the Berkeley unpacking\_dpo\_ppo
factorization (algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ vs stack axes)}. No prior ledger
row applied the Ivison 2024 framework to the N2 panel. We close that
recommendation by re-using the \texttt{axis\_variance\_fraction} helper
from \texttt{scripts/berkeley/unpacking\_dpo\_ppo\_factorization.py}
verbatim on the live N2 tensor table.

\subsubsection{Framework (Ivison 2024, NeurIPS)}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-ivison-framework}
Ivison et al.~(NeurIPS 2024, arXiv:2406.09279) decompose RL-from-feedback
pipelines into four axes: preference data, learning algorithm, reward
model, and policy-training prompts. For verifiable-reward RL (Tulu 3 RLVR,
arXiv:2411.15124) two of those axes are pinned by construction (data,
prompts); the remaining two (algorithm, reward model) become the
testable variance contributors. Ivison reports a decisive criterion:
the algorithm axis is \emph{under-identified} once the stack is pinned
when $\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}} = \mathrm{SS}_{\mathrm{algo}}/\mathrm{SS}_{\mathrm{total}}
\le 0.05$.

For the N2 four-method panel (\texttt{experiments/results/n2\_reward\_tensor\_resume/n2\_metrics.tsv},
160 rows = 4 methods $\times$ 40 steps $\times$ 1 seed on
Qwen2.5-0.5B-MATH), the data and prompts axes are pinned; we decompose
variance across the \emph{algorithm axis only}. We also report the
bias-corrected $\omega^2$ and a rolling 5-step per-step $\eta^2$ trajectory.

\subsubsection{Pooled algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ per channel (H1)}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-pooled}
Table~\ref{tab:p5-iter85-eta2} reports pooled $\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}}$
on every measured channel. Five of seven channels pass Ivison's strict
threshold ($\eta^2 \le 0.05$); six of seven pass the loose threshold
($\eta^2 \le 0.10$). The exception is \texttt{loss} ($\eta^2 = 0.987$),
a known method-specific signal (each GRPO-family variant has its own loss
landscape); it is a positive control, not a stack-conditioning channel.
The mean across the six non-loss channels is $\bar\eta^2 = 0.0331$.

\subsubsection{Per-step rolling $\eta^2$ trajectory (H2) and GIFT dominance (H3)}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-perstep}
A rolling 5-step window of algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ on \texttt{zvf}
shows a brief early-training spike ($\eta^2 \approx 0.224$ at step 0)
that decays to $\eta^2 < 0.10$ for steps $\ge 5$. \texttt{reward\_mean}
stays below $\eta^2 = 0.12$ on every step. The early-training spike is
\emph{exactly} the step window at which the iter-75 row 88 / iter-79
row 93 joint controller should not trust the algorithm axis.

GIFT dominates the algorithm axis: on the last-10-step pooled values,
Cohen's $d$ on \texttt{zvf} is $\mathbf{+1.899}$ (GIFT vs other three),
$\mathbf{-1.605}$ on \texttt{pcd}, and $\mathbf{+0.432}$ on
\texttt{reward\_mean}. The GIFT signal on \texttt{zvf} recovers the
iter-66 row 77 anti-herding $\delta_{\mathrm{div}}$ block at the raw
per-step layer: GIFT carries the structural diversity bonus measured in
row 77's empirical $[0.039, 0.053]$.

\subsubsection{Ivison equivalence on reward\_mean (H4)}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-h4}
All three algorithm pairs satisfy Ivison loose-equivalence
($|\Delta_{\mathrm{reward}}| \le 0.05$) on the last-10-step pooled
values: $|\Delta| = 0.0141$ (grpo-aero), $0.0195$ (grpo-areal),
$0.0164$ (grpo-gift). Strict Ivison ($|\Delta| \le 0.005$) is rejected
by $\approx 3\times$ on every pair. We therefore recommend adopting
\emph{loose} equivalence as the operational threshold for same-stack
multi-algorithm panels of GRPO-family methods.

\subsubsection{Cross-paper coupling}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-coupling}
\textbf{(i) P6 iter-66 row 77 / iter-74 row 87} --- the H3 GIFT
dominance reproduces iter-66's measured $\delta_{\mathrm{div}}$ at the
algorithm-axis layer; this row closes the missing \emph{algorithm}
axis on iter-66's \emph{reward} / \emph{zvf} pair.
\textbf{(ii) P7 iter-75 row 88 / iter-79 row 93} --- H2's per-step
$\eta^2$ trajectory identifies the early-training step-0 spike
($\eta^2_{\mathrm{zvf}} = 0.224$) as exactly where the joint
controller's calibration should not be trusted; the controller's
\texttt{zvf-triage} branch (which fires on \texttt{Y\_obs} $< 0.125$)
operates precisely on steps with low algorithm-axis $\eta^2$.
\textbf{(iii) Berkeley row 22} --- this row is the second empirical
anchor of the same \texttt{axis\_variance\_fraction} helper on a
different data panel. Samestack\_ppo\_grpo reports
$\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}} = 0.024$ on heldout\_acc; we recover
$\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}} = 0.0075$ on \texttt{reward\_mean} here.
Cross-panel replication at $<1\%$ algorithm-axis variance.
\textbf{(iv) P5 iter-65 row 76 / iter-73 row 86 / iter-80 row 95} ---
confirms the Pillar 1 stack-conditioning thesis at $\eta^2 \le 0.10$
on six of seven non-loss channels.

\subsubsection{Operational recommendation}
\label{sec:p5-iter85-op}
Report the N2 four-method same-stack panel as
\emph{algorithm-equivalent on 5 of 7 channels at $\eta^2 \le 0.05$}
and on \emph{all 6 non-loss channels at $\eta^2 \le 0.10$}.
Adopt Ivison loose-equivalence ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{reward}}| \le 0.05$)
as the operational threshold for ``same-stack multi-algorithm panel''
reporting, since strict Ivison is rejected by $\approx 3\times$ on
every reward pair. \subsection{Bootstrap CIs and leave-one-method-out stability on N2 algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ (iter 89)}
\label{sec:p5-iter89-n2-bootstrap}

The brief's vein (c) (\emph{bootstrap CIs on every P5 headline number})
was not closed by iter 85 row 101, which reported point estimates of
$\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}}$ on the same N2 panel without step-resampled
confidence bands. At $n{=}40$ steps the point estimates can be
sample-noise unstable, particularly on right-skewed channels like
\texttt{zvf} and \texttt{pcd}. We close the gap by re-using
\texttt{axis\_variance\_fraction} on a paired-step bootstrap
($B{=}4000$, seed 20260705), and add a leave-one-method-out (LOMO)
stability audit on the algorithm axis.

\subsubsection{Bootstrap CIs on pooled algorithm-axis $\eta^2$ (H1)}
\label{sec:p5-iter89-h1}
Table~\ref{tab:p5-iter89-boot} reports the 95\% bootstrap CIs on
$\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}}$ for each channel. \textbf{Only two of the
four channels that passed Ivison strict in iter 85 survive
bootstrap-strict}: \texttt{larq} (point 0.0010, UB 0.014) and
\texttt{reward\_mean} (point 0.0075, UB 0.024). The other two,
\texttt{zvf} (UB 0.113) and \texttt{pcd} (UB 0.081), exceed the
0.05 threshold under bootstrap. This is a direct \emph{correction}
of iter 85 row 101's strict-pass headline: at $n{=}40$ steps the
point estimate is below 0.05 by chance. Loose-Ivison (UB
$\leq 0.10$) survives on \texttt{pcd} (UB 0.081) and
\texttt{cv\_len} (UB 0.091), giving 4 of 7 channels (the same as
iter 85's \emph{loose} verdict). The \texttt{loss} channel stays
at $\eta^2{=}0.987$ (CI [0.983, 0.991]) as the positive control.

\subsubsection{Pair-wise algorithm-pair decomposition (H2)}
\label{sec:p5-iter89-h2}
We also decompose $\eta^2$ on every pair of methods (6 pairs) per
channel. The pattern is consistent across channels: the
\textbf{three non-GIFT pairs} (grpo-vs-aero, grpo-vs-areal,
aero-vs-areal) carry $\eta^2_{\mathrm{pair}} \leq 0.005$ with
bootstrap CI upper bound $\leq 0.04$ on \texttt{zvf} and \texttt{pcd}.
The \textbf{three GIFT-containing pairs} carry $\eta^2_{\mathrm{pair}}
\geq 0.04$ with CI upper bound 0.08--0.16 on the same channels. The
same pattern appears on \texttt{mean\_len} and \texttt{cv\_len}, and
\emph{only} \texttt{larq} and \texttt{reward\_mean} show all 6 pairs
below the 0.04 threshold. This is direct evidence that \textbf{the
algorithm-axis signal on \texttt{zvf}, \texttt{pcd}, \texttt{mean\_len},
and \texttt{cv\_len} is GIFT-driven, not a generic algorithm-axis
property}.

\subsubsection{Leave-one-method-out (LOMO) stability on \texttt{zvf} (H3)}
\label{sec:p5-iter89-h3}
Table~\ref{tab:p5-iter89-lomo} reports pooled $\eta^2_{\mathrm{algo}}$
on \texttt{zvf} after omitting one method at a time. Removing any of
the three non-GIFT methods yields $\eta^2 \in [0.043, 0.056]$ (CI
upper bound 0.11--0.14). \textbf{Removing GIFT drops $\eta^2$ to
0.0038, a $12\times$ reduction} (CI upper bound 0.039). GIFT is
uniquely load-bearing on the algorithm-axis signal: it carries
essentially all the between-method variance on \texttt{zvf}.
